However, in many applications, it is unrealistic to produce Haar random quantum states and partial derandomization is indispensable. Fortunately, quite often it suffices to use finite ensembles of quantum states that can match Haar random states up to the first t moments for some positive integer t, which leads to the concept of complex projective t-designs [ 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 , 5 , 6 , 7 ] . However, in many applications, it is unrealistic to produce Haar random quantum states and partial derandomization is indispensable. Fortunately, quite often it suffices to use finite ensembles of quantum states that can match Haar random states up to the first t moments for some positive integer t, which leads to the concept of complex projective t-designs [ 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 , 5 , 6 , 7 ] . Intuitively, a t-design is a (weighted) set of vectors on the complex unit sphere in a finite-dimensional Hilbert space \mathcal{H} that are “evenly distributed” , and a large value of t means a high degree of uniformity. Unitary t-designs are a natural generalization of complex projective t-designs to the unitary group [ 8 , 9 , 10 , 11 ] . They can replace Haar random unitaries in numerous applications and are equally useful in v

However, in many applications, it is unrealistic to produce Haar random quantum states and partial derandomization is indispensable. Fortunately, quite often it suffices to use finite ensembles of quantum states that can match Haar random states up to the first t moments for some positive integer t, which leads to the concept of complex projective t-designs [ 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 , 5 , 6 , 7 ] . Intuitively, a t-design is a (weighted) set of vectors on the complex unit sphere in a finite-dimensional Hilbert space \mathcal{H} that are “evenly distributed” , and a large value of t means a high degree of uniformity. Unitary t-designs are a natural generalization of complex projective t-designs to the unitary group [ 8 , 9 , 10 , 11 ] . They can replace Haar random unitaries in numerous applications and are equally useful in various research areas. In the past two decades, complex projective designs and unitary design have found increasing applications in quantum information processing, including randomized benchmarking [ 12 , 13 ] , quantum tomography [ 14 , 5 , 15 , 16 , 17 , 18 ] , quantum verification [ 19 , 2

However, in many applications, it is unrealistic to produce Haar random quantum states and partial derandomization is indispensable. Fortunately, quite often it suffices to use finite ensembles of quantum states that can match Haar random states up to the first t moments for some positive integer t, which leads to the concept of complex projective t-designs [ 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 , 5 , 6 , 7 ] . Intuitively, a t-design is a (weighted) set of vectors on the complex unit sphere in a finite-dimensional Hilbert space \mathcal{H} that are “evenly distributed” , and a large value of t means a high degree of uniformity. Unitary t-designs are a natural generalization of complex projective t-designs to the unitary group [ 8 , 9 , 10 , 11 ] . They can replace Haar random unitaries in numerous applications and are equally useful in various research areas. In the past two decades, complex projective designs and unitary design have found increasing applications in quantum information processing, including randomized benchmarking [ 12 , 13 ] , quantum tomography [ 14 , 5 , 15 , 16 , 17 , 18 ] , quantum verification [ 19 , 20 , 21 , 22 ] , quantum

To derive this lower bound we establish a simple connection between the shadow norm and a modified moment operator and then generalize a typical method for proving the familiar lower bound for the third frame potential. In Sec. III we first review basic concepts in the qudit stabilizer formalism, including the Heisenberg-Weyl group, Clifford Group, stabilizer codes, and stabilizer states. Then we review basic results about the commutant of the tth Clifford tensor power as developed in Refs. [ 64 , 68 ] and introduce the key concepts in this framework, such as the stochastic orthogonal group O_{t}(d), the set \Sigma_{t,t}(d) of stochastic Lagrangian subspaces, and the operators R(\mathcal{T}) for \mathcal{T}\in\Sigma_{t,t}(d), which span the commutant under certainty assumption. VI we clarify the basic properties of the third moment of the ensemble of n-qudit stabilizer states, denoted by \mathrm{Stab}(n,d) henceforth. Notably, we determine the spectrum and operator norm of the third normalized moment operator (see Proposition 3 and Table 4 ). It turns out the operator norm is upper bounded by (d+2)/3 when d=2\!\mod 3, but increases exponentially with n when d\neq 2\!\mod 3. The distinction between the two cases is tied to the different structures of stochastic Lagrangian subspaces as clarified in Sec. IV and has a number theoretic origin. . Notably, we determine the spectrum and operator norm of the third normalized moment operator (see Proposition 3 and Table 4 ). It turns out the operator norm is upper bounded by (d+2)/3 when d=2\!\mod 3, but increases exponentially with n when d\neq 2\!\mod 3. The distinction between the two cases is tied to the different structures of stochastic Lagrangian subspaces as clarified in Sec. IV and has a number theoretic origin. Furthermore, we determine the shadow norms of stabilizer projectors with respect to \mathrm{Stab}(n,d) and the shadow norm of \mathrm{Stab}(n,d) itself (see Theorems 3 and 4 ). It turns out the shadow norm of \mathrm{Stab}(n,d) is upper bounded by 2d-1, which is independent of n, although the deviation of \mathrm{Stab}(n,d) from a 3-design grows exponentially with n according to the operator norm of the third normalized moment operator when d\neq 2\!\mod 3. In Sec. VII we clarify the basic properties of a general Clifford orbit. To this end, we expand the third normalized moment operator in terms of the operator frame \{R(\mathcal{T})\}_{\mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d)} and a dual frame and clarify the basic properties of the expansion coefficients. On this basis we derive analytical formulas for the third normalized frame potential of the Clifford orbit and the shadow norm of any stabilizer projector. We also derive nearly tight bounds for the operator norm of t

projectors with respect to \mathrm{Stab}(n,d) and the shadow norm of \mathrm{Stab}(n,d) itself (see Theorems 3 and 4 ). It turns out the shadow norm of \mathrm{Stab}(n,d) is upper bounded by 2d-1, which is independent of n, although the deviation of \mathrm{Stab}(n,d) from a 3-design grows exponentially with n according to the operator norm of the third normalized moment operator when d\neq 2\!\mod 3. In Sec. VII we clarify the basic properties of a general Clifford orbit. To this end, we expand the third normalized moment operator in terms of the operator frame \{R(\mathcal{T})\}_{\mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d)} and a dual frame and clarify the basic properties of the expansion coefficients. On this basis we derive analytical formulas for the third normalized frame potential of the Clifford orbit and the shadow norm of any stabilizer projector. We also derive nearly tight bounds for the operator norm of the third normalized moment operator and the shadow norm of the Clifford orbit (see Theorems 5 - 9 ). It turns out the third normalized frame potential and shadow norm are both upper bounded by \mathcal{O}(d). The operator norm is upper bounded by \mathcal{O}(d) when d=2\!\mod 3, but may grow exponentially with n when d\neq 2\!\mod 3. The distinction between the two cases has the same origin as for the orbit of stabilizer states. In Sec. VIII we further clarify the properties of Cliff

VIII we further clarify the properties of Clifford orbits based on balanced ensembles (see Theorems 10 - 12 ). In Sec. IX we first review basic results about qudit diagonal magic gates, referred to as T gates henceforth. Then we turn to qudit magic states, which can be generated by applying the Fourier gate and T gates on the computational basis state. Both T gates and magic states are tied to cubic polynomials on the finite field \mathbb{F}_{d} (when d=3, the situation is slightly different). Furthermore, we introduce cubic characters to distinguish three types of T gates and three types of single-qudit magic states in the case d=1\!\mod 3. We also classify n-qudit magic states and highlight certain magic states that are of special interest. The existence of different types of T gates and magic states unravels a rich story yet to be fully understood. In Secs. X and XI we clarify the basic properties of magic orbits. Notably, we

 In Sec. VIII we further clarify the properties of Clifford orbits based on balanced ensembles (see Theorems 10 - 12 ). In Sec. IX we first review basic results about qudit diagonal magic gates, referred to as T gates henceforth. Then we turn to qudit magic states, which can be generated by applying the Fourier gate and T gates on the computational basis state. Both T gates and magic states are tied to cubic polynomials on the finite field \mathbb{F}_{d} (when d=3, the situation is slightly different). Furthermore, we introduce cubic characters to distinguish three types of T gates and three types of single-qudit magic states in the case d=1\!\mod 3. We also classify n-qudit magic states and highlight certain magic states that are of special interest. The existence of different types of T gates and magic states unravels a rich story yet to be fully understood. In Secs. X and XI we clarify the basic properties of magic orbits. Notably, we show that the third normalized frame potential, operator norm of the third normalized moment operator, and shadow norm all decrease exponentially with the number of T gates (see Theorems 13 - 21 ). Therefore, good approximate 3-designs with respect to the third normalized frame potential and shadow norm can be constructed using only a few T gates. Actually, a single T gate can already bridge the gap between qudit systems and qubit systems with 

malized moment operator, and shadow norm all decrease exponentially with the number of T gates (see Theorems 13 - 21 ). Therefore, good approximate 3-designs with respect to the third normalized frame potential and shadow norm can be constructed using only a few T gates. Actually, a single T gate can already bridge the gap between qudit systems and qubit systems with respect to the third normalized frame potential and shadow norm. The situation for the operator norm is more complicated because it may grow exponentially with n when d\neq 2\!\mod 3. Nevertheless, good approximate 3-designs with respect to all three figures of merit can be constructed from only two Clifford orbits (see Proposition 9 and Theorems 19 , 22 ; see Sec. X.3 for the special case d=3). Furthermore, for an infinite family of local dimensions, exact 3-designs can be constructed from only two or four Clifford orbits (see Lemma 31 , Theorem 27 , and Table 6 ). To establish the main results on magic orbits we first determine the expansion coefficients associated with magic states of a single qudit (see Lemmas 22 and 24 ). Technically, a key challenge is to count the number of solutions of a general cubic equation on each stochastic Lagrangian subspace, and one of our main contributions is to tackle this challenge after simplifying the counting problem. Our insight on stochastic Lagrangian subspaces plays a crucial role in this endeavor. Incidentally, most sophisticated analysis is required to clarify the properties of the operator norm of the third normalized moment operator compared with the third normalized frame potential and shadow norm. Although this work was originally motivated by shadow estimation, the scope and implications of this work have gone far beyond the original motivation. y a linear combination of permutation operators. Recently, Gross, Nezami, and Walter (GNW) proved a theorem similar in flavor for the Clifford group [ 68 ] . Their work is the basis for understanding the third moments of Clifford orbits. Here we recapitulate their main results that are relevant to the current study, assuming that the local dimension d is an odd prime. III.3.1 Stochastic Lagrangian subspaces and a spanning set of the commutant Let \mathbf{1}_{2t}=(1,1,\dots,1)^{\top} be the column vector in \mathbb{F}_{d}^{2t} whose entries are all equal to 1. A subspace \mathcal{T}\leq\mathbb{F}_{d}^{2t} (the inequality means \mathcal{T} is a subspace of \mathbb{F}_{d}^{2t}) is a stochastic Lagrangian subspace if it satisfies the following three conditions [ 68 ] : 1. \mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\cdot\mathbf{y}=0 for any (\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})\in\mathcal{T}. 2. \mathcal{T} has dimension t. 3. \mathbf{1}_{2t}\in\mathcal{T}. Here \mathbf{x} and \mathbf{y} are regarded as column vectors in \mathbb{F}_{d}^{t}, and (\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y}) is regarded as a column vector in \mathbb{F}_{d}^{2t} obtained by concatenating \mathbf{x} and \mathbf{y} vertically [in contrast with the notation (\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}), which means horizontal concatenation]; all arithmetic are modulo d. Condition 1 means the subspace \mathcal{T} is totally isotropic with respect to the quadratic form \

Sigma_{t,t}(d)} spans the commutant of \mathrm{Cl}(n,d)^{\tilde{\otimes}t} when n\geq t-1. GNW also conjectured that the condition n\geq t-1 is not necessary. We shall prove their speculation for the special case of t=3 in Sec. V.2 A spanning set and dual frame By virtue of Proposition 2 , here we show that the set \{R(\mathcal{T})\}_{\mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d)} spans the commutant of the third Clifford tensor power. When n\geq 2, this conclusion was already established by GNW [ 68 ] . Our analysis shows that the restriction n\geq 2 is not necessary. The following theorem is proved in Appendix D.2 . Theorem 2 . Suppose d is an odd prime and n\in\mathbb{N}. Then \{R(\mathcal{T})\}_{\mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d)} spans the commutant of \mathrm{Cl}(n,d)^{\tilde{\otimes}3}. If in addition n\geq 2, then \{R(\mathcal{T})\}_{\mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d)} forms a basis of the commutant; in the case n=1, any subset of \{R(\mathcal{T})\}_{\mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d)} with cardinality 2d+1 forms a basis of the commutant. When n\geq 2, \{R(\mathcal{T})\}_{\mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d)} forms a basis of the commutant of \mathrm{Cl}(n,d)^{\tilde{\otimes}3} thanks to Theorem 2 . By virtue of Proposition 2 , we can further determine the dual basis. Define \displaystyle\tilde{R}(\mathcal{T}):=\frac{1}{D^{3}-D}\left[R(\mathcal{T})+\frac{d}{D^{2}-d^{2}}R(\mathscr{T}_{j})-\frac{D}{D^{2}-d^{2}}R(\mathscr{T}_{\bar{j}})\right]\quad\forall\mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{T}_{j}, (102) where \bar{j}=1-j. Then \{\tilde{R}(\mathcal{T})\}_{\mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d)} is the dual basis of \{R(\mathcal{T})\}_{\math

Suppose d is a prime, then the spectrum of \bar{Q}(n,d,3) within the symmetric subspace \mathrm{Sym}_{3}\bigl(\mathcal{H}_{d}^{\otimes n}\bigr) is given in Table 4 . When d=2\!\mod 3, the operator \bar{Q}(n,d,3) is proportional to a projector. When d=3 or d=1\!\mod 3, by contrast, \bar{Q}(n,d,3) has two distinct nonzero eigenvalues. When d=2 or d=3, \bar{Q}(n,d,3) has full rank in \mathrm{Sym}_{3}\bigl(\mathcal{H}_{d}^{\otimes n}\bigr). When d\geq 5, by contrast, \bar{Q}(n,d,3) is rank deficient; moreover, the ratio \operatorname{rank}(\bar{Q}(n,d,3))/\pi_{[3]} decreases monotonically with n and approaches the following limit when n\to\infty, \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\operatorname{rank}(\bar{Q}(n,d,3))}{\pi_{[3]}}=\begin{cases}\frac{6}{2d+2}\quad&\text{if}\ d=2\!\mod 3,\\ \frac{6}{2d-2}\quad&\text{if}\ d=1\!\mod 3.\end{cases} (116) By virtue of Proposition 3 , it is easy to compute the Schatten \ell-norm of \bar{Q}(n,d,3) for \ell\geq 1, in which the operator norm is presented in Table 4 .  unbiased bases (MUB) [ 70 ] . If \mathfrak{O} is the projector onto a basis state in the MUB, then Theorem 3 implies that \displaystyle\|\mathfrak{O}_{0}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{Stab}} \displaystyle=\frac{(d^{2}-1)(d-1)}{d^{2}}\leq d-1,\quad\frac{\|\mathfrak{O}_{0}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{Stab}}}{\|\mathfrak{O}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}}=\frac{d^{2}-1}{d}\leq d. (130) If instead \mathfrak{O}\in\mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathcal{H}_{d}), then Theorem 4 implies that \displaystyle\!\!\!\frac{(d+1)\|\mathfrak{O}\|_{2}^{2}}{d}\leq\|\mathfrak{O}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{Stab}}\leq\frac{(d+1)[(2d-3)\|\mathfrak{O}\|_{2}^{2}+2\|\mathfrak{O}\|^{2}]}{2d}\leq\frac{(d+1)(2d-1)}{2d}\|\mathfrak{O}\|_{2}^{2}\leq(d+1)\|\mathfrak{O}\|_{2}^{2}, (131) \displaystyle\frac{d^{2}-1}{d}\leq\|\mathrm{Stab}(n,d)\|_{\mathrm{sh}}\leq\frac{(d+1)(2d-1)}{2d}\leq d+1. (132) VII Third moment of a general Clifford orbit In this section we clarify the basic properties of the third moment of a general Clifford orbit, assuming that the local dimension d is an odd prime. We show that the third normalized frame potential and shadow norm of any Clifford orbit are \mathcal{O}(d) irrespective of the number n of qudits. If d=2\!\mod 3, then the same conclusion applies to the operator norm of the third normalized moment operator; if instead d\neq 2\mod 3, however, then this norm may grow exponentially with n. To this end, we derive exact formulas or nearly tight bounds

(132) VII Third moment of a general Clifford orbit In this section we clarify the basic properties of the third moment of a general Clifford orbit, assuming that the local dimension d is an odd prime. We show that the third normalized frame potential and shadow norm of any Clifford orbit are \mathcal{O}(d) irrespective of the number n of qudits. If d=2\!\mod 3, then the same conclusion applies to the operator norm of the third normalized moment operator; if instead d\neq 2\mod 3, however, then this norm may grow exponentially with n. To this end, we derive exact formulas or nearly tight bounds for the three figures of merit. The following proposition clarifies the symmetry of \kappa(\Psi,\mathcal{T}) and \hat{\kappa}(\Psi,\mathcal{T}); it is a simple corollary of the fact that R(O) for each O\in S_{3} is a permutation. Proposition 4 . Suppose d is an odd prime, |\Psi\rangle\in\mathcal{H}_{d}^{\otimes n}, and \mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d). Then \displaystyle\kappa(\Psi,\mathcal{T}) \displaystyle=\kappa(\Psi,O\mathcal{T})=\kappa(\Psi,\mathcal{T}O)\quad\forall O\in S_{3}, (140) and this equation still holds if \kappa is replaced by |\kappa|, \hat{\kappa}, or |\hat{\kappa}| or if \mathcal{T} is replaced by any subset \mathscr{T} of \Sigma(d). VIII Clifford orbits based on balanced ensembles Let \mathscr{E}=\{|\Psi_{j}\rangle,p_{j}\}_{j} be an ensemble of pure states in \mathcal{H}_{d}^{\otimes n}, where \{p_{j}\}_{j} forms a probability distribution. Let \mathrm{orb}(\mathscr{E}) be the ensemble constructed from \mathrm{orb}(\Psi_{j}) according to the probability distribution \{p_{j}\}_{j}. Then the third normalized moment operator of \mathrm{orb}(\mathscr{E}) reads \displaystyle\bar{Q}(\mathrm{orb}(\mathscr{E}))=\sum_{j}p_{j}\bar{Q}(\mathrm{orb}(\Psi_{j}))=\frac{1}{6}\sum_{\mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d)}\hat{\kappa}(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T})R(\mathcal{T}), (183) where \bar{Q}(\mathrm{orb}(\mathscr{E})) and \bar{Q}(\mathrm{orb}(\Psi_{j})) are abbreviations of \bar{Q}_{3}(\mathrm{orb}(\maths

l{T}). (184) Most results on \kappa(\Psi,\mathcal{T}) and \hat{\kappa}(\Psi,\mathcal{T}), including Propositions 4 , 5 and Lemma 20 for example, also apply to \kappa(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T}) and \hat{\kappa}(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T}). The ensemble \mathscr{E}=\{|\Psi_{j}\rangle,p_{j}\}_{j} is balanced if \kappa(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T}) for all \mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{ns}} are equal to each other. In this case, \hat{\kappa}(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T}) for all \mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{ns}} are also equal to each other according to Proposition 5 . In view of this fact, \kappa(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T}) and \hat{\kappa}(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T}) for \mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{ns}} can be abbreviated as \kappa(\mathscr{E}) and \hat{\kappa}(\mathscr{E}), respectively, when there is no danger of confusion. For example, any ensemble composed of one or more stabilizer states is balanced thanks to Proposition 4 . A balanced ensemble \mathscr{E} is interesting because the third moment of \mathrm{orb}(\mathscr{E}) is quite simple, which may serve as a benchmark for understanding more general ensembles. In addition, such ensembles are very useful to constructing 3-designs as we shall see in Sec. XI.5 . The following lemma is proved in Appendix G . Lemma 21 . Suppose d is an odd prime and \mathscr{E} is a balanced ensemble on \mathcal{H}_{d}^{\otimes n}. Then \displayst

(193) IX Qudit magic gates and magic states In this section we review basic results about qudit magic gates and magic states, where the local dimension d is an odd prime. By convention, these magic gates belong to the third Clifford hierarchy [ 47 ] . We are mainly interested in magic gates that are diagonal in the computational basis, which were clarified by Howard and Vala [ 92 ] . When d>3, let \tilde{\mathscr{P}}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{d}) be the set of cubic polynomials on \mathbb{F}_{d} with nonzero cubic coefficients; when d=3, let \til

mod 3. (194) In either case, the special cubic polynomial/function f(u)=u^{3} is called canonical . Proposition 7 . Suppose d is an odd prime. Then a diagonal unitary U is a magic gate iff it has the form U=\operatorname{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi}T_{f}, where \phi is a real phase, f\in\tilde{\mathscr{P}}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{d}), and \displaystyle T_{f}:=\sum_{u\in\mathbb{F}_{d}}\tilde{\omega}^{f(u)}|u\rangle\langle u|,\quad\tilde{\omega}:=\begin{cases}\omega_{d}=\operatorname{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}/d}&d\geq 5,\\ \omega_{9}=\operatorname{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}/9}&d=3.\end{cases} (195) The overall phase factor \operatorname{e}^{\mathrm{i}\phi} in Proposition 7 is irrelevant to our study and will be ignored in the following discussion. The diagonal magic gate T_{f} defined in Proposition 7 will be referred to as a T gate henceforth. The single-qudit magic state associated with T_{f} reads \displaystyle|\psi_{f}\rangle:=T_{f}|+\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{u\in\mathbb{F}_{d}}\tilde{\omega}^{f(u)}|u\rangle, (196) where |+\rangle:=\sum_{u\in\mathbb{F}_{d}}|u\rangle/\sqrt{d}. By definition |\psi_{f}\rangle can be generated from |0\rangle by the Fourier gate F (which belongs to the Clifford group) followed by the magic gate T_{f}. The magic gate T_{f} and magic state |\psi_{f}\rangle are canonical if f is canonical. Define \mathscr{M}(d) as the set of all magic states in \mathcal{H}_{d} that have the f

XI.1 Technical basis To understand the basic properties of the Clifford orbit generated from a magic state |\Psi\rangle\in\mathscr{M}_{n,k}(d), we need to determine the function \kappa(\Psi,\mathcal{T}) defined in Eq. ( 135 ). To this end, first we need to consider the case in which |\Psi\rangle is a single-qudit magic state. Then |\Psi\rangle is specified by a cubic polynomial f\in\tilde{\mathscr{P}}_{3}(\mathbb{F}_{d}) according to Eq. ( 196 ), and \kappa(\Psi,\mathcal{T}) can be written as \kappa(f,\mathcal{T}). Theorem 22 and Fig. 10 in Sec. XI.3 ). \frac{4^{k}}{d^{k-1}}. (261) Theorem 21 is a simple corollary of Theorem 9 and Lemma 28 . Thanks to Theorem 21 and Lemma 26 , the shadow norm \|\mathrm{orb}(\Psi)\|_{\mathrm{sh}} converges to 3 exponentially as k increases; if k\geq 2, then \|\mathrm{orb}(\Psi)\|_{\mathrm{sh}} converges to 3 as d increases too. XI.3 Construction of more accurate approximate 3-designs Here we propose a simple recipe for constructing accurate approximate 3-designs with respect to three figures of merit, including the operator norm of the third normalized moment operator. Only two Clifford orbits are required to apply this recipe. Let \mathscr{E} be an ensemble of pure states in \mathcal{H}_{d}^{\otimes n} and define \displaystyle\kappa_{\min}(\mathscr{E}):=\min_{\mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d)}\kappa(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T}),\quad\hat{\kappa}_{\min}(\mathscr{E}):=\min_{\mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d)}\hat{\kappa}(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T}). (262) Let \mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{def}} be any given stochastic Lagrangian subspace in \mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{def}}; then \kappa(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T})=\kappa(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{def}}) for all \mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{def}} by Proposition 4 and Lemma 5 . Suppose \mathscr{E} satisfies the following two equivalent conditions [cf. Proposition 5 ], \displaystyle\kappa_{\min}(\mathscr{E})=\kappa(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{def}})\leq\frac{\kappa(\mathscr{E}

=\kappa(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{def}}) for all \mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{def}} by Proposition 4 and Lemma 5 . Suppose \mathscr{E} satisfies the following two equivalent conditions [cf. Proposition 5 ], \displaystyle\kappa_{\min}(\mathscr{E})=\kappa(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{def}})\leq\frac{\kappa(\mathscr{E},\Sigma(d))}{2(D+d)},\quad\min_{\mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d)}\hat{\kappa}(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T})=\hat{\kappa}(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{def}})\leq 0. (263) Let \mathscr{E}_{p} be the ensemble constructed by choosing the ensembles \mathscr{E} and \{|0\rangle^{\otimes n}\} with probabilities 1-p and p respectively. Then the normalized moment operator of \mathrm{orb}(\mathscr{E}_{p}) reads \begin{gathered}\bar{Q}(\mathrm{orb}(\mathscr{E}_{p}))=(1-p)\bar{Q}(\mathrm{orb}(\mathscr{E}))+p\bar{Q}(n,d,3)=\frac{1}{6}\sum_{\mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d)}\left[\hat{\kappa}(\mathscr{E}_{p},\mathcal{T})R(\mathcal{T})\right],\\ \hat{\kappa}(\mathscr{E}_{p},\mathcal{T}):=(1-p)\hat{\kappa}(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T})+\frac{p(D+2)}{D+d},\end{gathered} (264) where \bar{Q}(n,d,3) is the third normalized moment operator of stabilizer states. Here \hat{\kappa}(\mathscr{E}_{p},\mathcal{T}) enjoys the same symmetry of \kappa(\Psi,\mathcal{T}) as clarified in Proposition 4 . XI.5 Construction of exact 3-designs Suppose \mathscr{E} is a balanced ensemble and satisfies the conditions in Eq. ( 263 ), which can now be simplified as \displaystyle\kappa(\mathscr{E})\leq\frac{3}{D+2}, (297) which is equivalent to the condition \hat{\kappa}(\mathscr{E})\leq 0. Let p be the probability determined by Eq. ( 265 ). Then \mathscr{E}_{p} is a balanced ensemble with \hat{\kappa}(\mathscr{E}_{p})=0, so \mathrm{orb}(\mathscr{E}_{p}) forms an exact 3-design by Eq. ( 269 ) (cf. Proposition 6 ). Next, suppose \mathscr{E} is a k-balanced magic ensemble. Then \kappa(\mathscr{E})=\kappa_{d,k} by Lemma 29 , where \kappa_{d,k} is defined in Eq. ( 278 ) and decreases monotonically with k given that 1\leq\tilde{g}(d)^{2}\leq 4d-27 by Eq. ( 235 ). Define \displaystyle k_{*}(d,n):=\min\left\{k\in\mathbb{N}\,\Big|\,\kappa_{d,k}\leq\frac{3}{D+2}\right\},\quad\mathscr{S}(d):=\{n\,|\,k_{*}(d,n)\leq n\}; (298) note that we do not impose the requirement k\leq n in the definition of k_{*}(d,n). Then the condition in Eq. ( 297 ) is satisfied iff k_{*}(d,n)\leq k\leq n. If this condition holds, then n\in\mathscr{S}(d) and an exact 3-design can be constructed from a k-balanced magic ensemble. On the other hand, if \kappa_{d,n}>3/(D+2), then k_{*}(d,n)\geq n+1, 

a}(\mathscr{E}_{p})=0, so \mathrm{orb}(\mathscr{E}_{p}) forms an exact 3-design by Eq. ( 269 ) (cf. Proposition 6 ). Next, suppose \mathscr{E} is a k-balanced magic ensemble. Then \kappa(\mathscr{E})=\kappa_{d,k} by Lemma 29 , where \kappa_{d,k} is defined in Eq. ( 278 ) and decreases monotonically with k given that 1\leq\tilde{g}(d)^{2}\leq 4d-27 by Eq. ( 235 ). Define \displaystyle k_{*}(d,n):=\min\left\{k\in\mathbb{N}\,\Big|\,\kappa_{d,k}\leq\frac{3}{D+2}\right\},\quad\mathscr{S}(d):=\{n\,|\,k_{*}(d,n)\leq n\}; (298) note that we do not impose the requirement k\leq n in the definition of k_{*}(d,n). Then the condition in Eq. ( 297 ) is satisfied iff k_{*}(d,n)\leq k\leq n. If this condition holds, then n\in\mathscr{S}(d) and an exact 3-design can be constructed from a k-balanced magic ensemble. On the other hand, if \kappa_{d,n}>3/(D+2), then k_{*}(d,n)\geq n+1, n\notin\mathscr{S}(d), and exact 3-designs cannot be constructed in this way for all k\leq n. Direct calculation based on Eqs. ( 278 ) and ( 298 ) yields \displaystyle k_{*}(d,n)=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{if}\quad n=1,\\ 3&\mbox{if}\quad n=2,\\ 3&\mbox{if}\quad n=3,\;\tilde{g}(d)^{2}\leq 3d^{4}/(d^{3}+2),\\ 4&\mbox{if}\quad n=3,\;\tilde{g}(d)^{2}>3d^{4}/(d^{3}+2).\end{cases} (299) So the condition in Eq. ( 297 ) is satisfied when k=n=1, but cannot be satisfied when n=2, which means 1\in\mathscr{S}(d) and 2\notin\mathscr{S

Next, we consider the asymptotic behavior of the set \mathscr{S}(d) as d gets large. Note that the value of \tilde{g}(d)^{2} is tied to the phase \phi of the Gauss sum G(\eta_{3}) according to Eq. ( 235 ). The distribution of the phase \phi was originally studied by Kummer in the 19th century, who formulated the famous Kummer conjecture (falsified later). Now, it is well known that the phase of the Gauss sum G(\eta_{3}) is asymptotically uniformly distributed in the interval [0,2\pi) if we consider all primes d with d=1\!\mod 3, although there is a bias if we set an upper bound for the primes [ 86 ] . The bias in the distribution remains an active research topic in number theory until now [ 87 ] . Asympt

XII Summary When the local dimension d is an odd prime, the qudit Clifford group is only a 2-design, but not a 3-design, which is in sharp contrast with the counterpart based on a qubit system. This distinction extends to the orbit of stabilizer states and other generic Clifford orbits. To clarify the imp

st claim in Lemma B1 . If O\in O_{3}(d)\setminus S_{3}, then O has no entry equal to 0 according to Proposition B1 . If O\in S_{3}, then it is straightforward to verify that any row of O-\mathds{1} is a linear combination of the other two rows. If O\in O_{3}^{\mathrm{even}}(d)\setminus S_{3}, then \ker(O-\mathds{1})=\operatorname{span}(\mathbf{1}_{3}) according to Lemma B1 . So the row span of O-\mathds{1} is two dimensional, and the three rows of O-\mathds{1} are linearly dependent. On the other hand, the three rows of O-\mathds{1} are related to each other by cyclic permutations according to Eq. ( 324 ), so any two rows must be linearly independent. Therefore, any row of O-\mathds{1} is a linear combination of the other two rows. If O\in O_{3}^{\mathrm{odd}}(d), then \dim\ker(O-\mathds{1})=2 according to Lemma B1 , so the row span of O-\mathds{1} is one dimensional. If in addition O\in O_{3}^{\mathrm{odd}}(d)\setminus S_{3}, then no entry of O-\mathds{1} is equal to 0 according to Proposition B1 . Therefore, the three rows of O-\mathds{1} are nonzero vectors that are proportional to each other, which means any row of O-\mathds{1} is a linear combination of the other two rows. Suppose d is an odd prime and a,b,c\in\mathbb{F}_{d} satisfy a+b+c=0. Then a^{3}+b^{3}+c^{3}=3abc. If in addition d\geq 5, then a^{3}+b^{3}+c^{3}=0 iff abc=0, that is, one of the three numbers a,b,c is equal to 0. Proof of Lemma C2 . Lemma C2 is a simple corollary of the following equation, \displaystyle a^{3}+b^{3}+c^{3}-3abc=\frac{3(a+b+c)(a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2})-(a+b+c)^{3}}{2}=0. (340) ∎ Proof of Lemma 9 . By assumption v_{1}+v_{2}+v_{3}=\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{3}=0, so the equality 3v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}=v_{1}^{3}+v_{2}^{3}+v_{3}^{3} follows from Lemma C2 . As a corollary, v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}=0 iff v_{1}^{3}+v_{2}^{3}+v_{3}^{3}=0 when d\geq 5. The equivalence of the three conditions in Eq. ( 82 ) follows from ( 80 ). The equivalence of the first two conditions in Eq. ( 83 ) follows from Eqs. ( 73 ) and ( 80 ) given that \mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{sym}}\cap\mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{odd}}=\mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{sym}}\cap\mathscr{T}_{1}. If v_{1}v_{2}v_{3}=0, then one of the three entries v_{1},v_{2},v_{3} is zero, and \mathbf{v} is proportional to (1,-1,0)^{\top}, (1,0,-1)^{\top}, or (0,1,-1)^{\top}. So \mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{v}\neq 0 and O_{\mathbf{v}} is a tran

\setminus S_{3}, and we have \mathbf{x}=O\mathbf{y} and \mathbf{y}=O^{\top}\mathbf{x}. According to Proposition B1 , O has no entry equal to 0, so (\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y}) has at most two entries equal to zero given that (\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y}) is a nonzero vector by assumption. Next, suppose \mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d)\setminus\mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{iso}}=\mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{def}}; then \mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}^{\perp} and \mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{RD}}^{\perp}. According to Lemma C5 , any nonzero vector in \mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{LD}} or \mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{RD}} has no entry equal to 0, while any nonzero vector in \mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{LD}}^{\perp} or \mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{RD}}^{\perp} has at most one entry equal to 0. If \mathbf{x} has at least two entries equal to 0, then \mathbf{x}=0, and \mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{RD}} has no entry equal to 0. Similarly, if \mathbf{y} has at least two entries equal to 0, then \mathbf{y}=0, and \mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{LD}} has no entry equal to 0. Therefore, (\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y}) has at most three entries equal to 0. If the upper bound is saturated, then either \mathbf{x} or \mathbf{y} has no entry equal to 0. As a simple corollary of the above discussion, any nonzero vector in \mathcal{T}_{\Delta} has at most two zero entries, given that the number of zero entries is necessarily even. ∎ Proof of Lemma C7 . ( 124 ) and ( 126 ) thanks to the formula in Eq. ( 119 ). The equivalence of the first three statements in Lemma F3 is a simple corollary of Lemma F1 . The equivalence of statements 3 and 4 follows from Proposition 5 and the fact that \kappa(\mathcal{T})\leq\kappa(\Delta)=1 for all \mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d) by Lemma 20 . ( 192 ). ∎ Appendix H Gauss sums and Jacobi sums Here we review some basic results on Gauss sums and Jacobi sums that are relevant to the current study. To this end we first need to review a few basic concepts about multiplicative characters of finite fields. Our discussion is based on Refs. [ 93 , 94 ] . H.1 Multiplicative characters of \mathbb{F}_{d} Suppose d is a prime. A multiplicative character of the field \mathbb{F}_{d

beys an important quadratic reciprocity relation as stated in the following lemma, which reproduces Theorem 5.17 in Ref. [ 93 ] . Lemma H1 . Suppose p and s are two distinct odd primes. Then \eta_{2}(p,s)\eta_{2}(s,p)=(-1)^{(p-1)(s-1)/4}. An element in \mathbb{F}_{d} is a cubic residue if it is the cube of another element and a cubic nonresidue otherwise. When d\neq 1\mod 3, every element in \mathbb{F}_{d} is a cubic residue. When d=1\!\mod 3, the situation is different: 0 and one third elements in \mathbb{F}_{d}^{\times} are cubic residues. A multiplicative character \eta of \mathbb{F}_{d} is a cubic character if it has order 3. In that case, \eta(a)=1 iff a is a nonzero cubic residue. In addition, \eta corresponds to the character in Eq. ( 514 ) with j=(d-1)/3 or j=2(d-1)/3, and the first choice means \eta(\nu^{k})=\omega_{3}^{k}, where \omega_{3}=\operatorname{e}^{2\pi\mathrm{i}/3}. This cubic character depends on the choice of the primitive element \nu, in contrast with \eta_{2}, which is independent of \nu. H.2 Gauss sums Here we review two types of Gauss sums that are crucial to the current study. Let m>1 be a positive integer, a\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{\times}, and let \eta be a nontrivial multiplicative character of \mathbb{F}_{d} of order m. The Gauss sums g(m,a) and G(\eta,a) [ 93 , 94 ] are defined as \displaystyle g(m,a):=\sum_{b\in\mathbb{F}_{d}}\omega_{d}^{ab^{m}},\quad

), respectively, and similarly for g(m,a). Next, we summarize a few basic properties of Gauss sums. Proposition H1 below follows from Theorems 1.1.3 and 1.1.4 in Ref. [ 94 ] ; see also Theorems 5.11 and 5.12 in Ref. [ 93 ] . Proposition H1 . Suppose d is an odd prime, a\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{\times}, and \eta is a nontrivial multiplicative character of \mathbb{F}_{d}. Then \displaystyle|G(\eta,a)|=\sqrt{d},\quad G(\eta,a)=\eta(1/a)G(\eta)=\eta^{*}(a)G(\eta),\quad G^{*}(\eta,a)=\eta(-1)G(\eta^{*},a). (518) Now suppose m>1 is a positive integer that divides d-1 and \eta is a multiplicative character of \mathbb{F}_{d} of order m. As a simple corollary of Eq. [ 93 ] we can deduce that \displaystyle N_{c}(f) \displaystyle=N(x^{3}+y^{3}=c)=d+\sum_{j_{1},j_{2}=1}^{2}\eta_{3}^{j_{1}}(c)\eta_{3}^{j_{2}}(c)J\bigl(\eta_{3}^{j_{1}},\eta_{3}^{j_{2}}\bigr)=d+2J\bigl(\eta_{3},\eta_{3}^{2}\bigr)+\sum_{j=1}^{2}\eta_{3}^{2j}(c)J\bigl(\eta_{3}^{j},\eta_{3}^{j}\bigr) \displaystyle=d-2+2\real\eta_{3}^{2}(c)J(\eta_{3},\eta_{3}), (546) where the last equality follows from Eqs. ( 534 ) and ( 535 ). This equation confirms Eq. ( 544 ) and implies Eq. ( 545 ) given that |\eta_{3}^{2}(c)J(\eta_{3},\eta_{3})|=|J(\eta_{3},\eta_{3})|=\sqrt{d} by Lemma H4 . ∎ Lemma I4 . J.1 Main results Suppose d is an odd prime and let f be a function from \mathbb{F}_{d} to \mathbb{F}_{d}, that is, f\in\mathbb{F}_{d}[x]; then f can be extended to a function from \mathbb{F}_{d}^{3} to \mathbb{F}_{d} as follows, \displaystyle f(\mathbf{x}):=f(x_{1})+f(x_{2})+f(x_{3}),\quad\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})^{\top}\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{3}. (549) Given f\in\mathbb{F}_{d}[x], \mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d), and \alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{d}, define N_{\alpha}(f,\mathcal{T}) as the number of solutions to the equation f(\mathbf{y})-f(\mathbf{x})=\alpha for (\mathbf{x};\mathbf{y})\in\mathcal{T}, that is, \displaystyle N_{\alpha}(f,\mathcal{T}):=|\{(\mathbf{x};\ma

ation also holds for all \mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d) when d=3 and f is a cubic polynomial, which can be verified by virtue of Lemma 4 . In general, it is not easy to determine N_{\alpha}(f,\mathcal{T}) when \mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{ns}}, where \displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{ns}}=\Sigma(d)\setminus\mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{sym}}=(\mathscr{T}_{1}\setminus\mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{sym}})\sqcup[(\mathscr{T}_{1}\setminus\mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{sym}})\tau_{12}]. (553) Here our main goal is to determine N_{\alpha}(f,\mathcal{T}) when f is a cubic polynomial. For the convenience of the following discussion, the special cubic polynomial f(x)=x^{3} is referred to as the the canonical cubic polynomial. When d\geq 5, this definition agrees with the definition in Sec. IX ; when d=3, however, the canonical cubic polynomial is different from the canonical cubic function defined in Sec. IX . When f is the canonical cubic polynomial, N_{\alpha}(f,\mathcal{T}) is abbreviated as N_{\alpha}(\mathcal{T}) for simplicity. Lemma J1 . q. ( 568 ), given that |\eta_{3}(3c/\alpha)|=1 and |J(\eta_{3},\eta_{3})|=|G(\eta_{3})|=\sqrt{d} by Proposition H1 and Lemma H4 . ∎ J.2 Two auxiliary lemmas Lemma J4 . Suppose d is a prime; a,b,c\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{\times} and x,y,z\in\mathbb{F}_{d} satisfy the following conditions, \displaystyle a+b+c=0=xa+yb+zc=x^{2}a+y^{2}b+z^{2}c=0. (571) Then x=y=z. Proof of Lemma J4 . Let \displaystyle A=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1\\ x&y&z\\ x^{2}&y^{2}&z^{2}\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathbf{v}=\begin{pmatrix}a\\ b\\ c\end{pmatrix}. (572) Then the assumption in Eq. ( 571 ) can be expressed as A\mathbf{v}=0, which implies that \displaystyle\det(A)=(x-y)(y-z)(z-x)=0, (573) given that a,b,c\in\mathbb{F}_{d}^{\times}. So at least two of the three numbers x,y,z must be equal, say x=y. If x=y\neq z, then \ker(A)=\operatorname{span}(1;-1;0) cannot contain any vector that is composed of three nonzero entries. Similarly, \ker(A) cannot contain any vector that is composed of three nonzero entries if x=z\neq y or y=z\neq x. Therefore, x=y=z. ∎ Lemma J5 . Let \displaystyle a=v_{1}^{3}+v_{2}^{3}+v_{3}^{3},\quad b=-3\bigl(u_{1}^{2}v_{1}+u_{2}^{2}v_{2}+u_{3}^{2}v_{3}\bigr); (574) then

Suppose |\Psi\rangle\in\mathscr{M}_{n,k}(d); then \kappa(\Psi) is completely determined by \mathscr{C}_{l}(\Psi) for l=0,1,2. To prove Lemma 29 , therefore, it suffices to consider any given k-balanced ensemble, say the ensemble defined in Eq. ( 277 ). If k=0\!\mod 3, then \displaystyle\kappa(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T})=\kappa(\tilde{\Psi},\mathcal{T})=[\kappa(f_{0},\mathcal{T})\kappa(f_{1},\mathcal{T})\kappa(f_{2},\mathcal{T})]^{\lfloor k/3\rfloor}=\frac{\tilde{g}(d)^{2\lfloor k/3\rfloor}}{d^{k+\lfloor k/3\rfloor}}, (658) where the last equality follows from Lemma 25 given that \{\kappa(f_{j},\mathcal{T})\}_{j=0,1,2} is identical to \{\kappa_{j}^{\uparrow}\}_{j=0,1,2} up to a permutation. If k=1\!\mod 3, then \displaystyle\kappa(\mathscr{E},\mathcal{T})=\frac{\kappa(\Psi_{0},\mathcal{T})+\kappa(\Psi_{1},\mathcal{T})+\kappa(\Psi_{2},\mathcal{T})}{3}=\frac{\kappa(\tilde{\Psi},\mathcal{T})[\kappa(f_{0},\mathcal{T})+\kappa(f_{1},\mathcal{T})+\kappa(f_{2},\mathcal{T})]}{3}=\frac{2\tilde{g}(d)^{2\lfloor k/3\rfloor}}{d^{k+\lfloor k/3\rfloor}}, (659) given that \kappa(f_{0},\mathcal{T})+\kappa(f_{1},\mathcal{T})+\kappa(f_{2},\mathcal{T})=6/d by Lemma 25 . If k=2\!\mod 3, then \displaystyle\kapp

ws from the fact that D=d^{n} and \kappa(\Psi,\Sigma(d))\leq 21/2 by Lemma 28 . In conjunction with Proposition 5 the above equation means \hat{\kappa}(\Psi,\mathcal{T})\geq 0 for all \mathcal{T}\in\mathscr{T}_{\mathrm{ns}}, which implies the equality in Eq. ( 285 ). The inequalities in Eq. ( 285 ) follow from Lemmas F6 and 29 . If k=2\!\mod 3, then |\Psi\rangle can be expressed as a tensor product of the form |\Psi\rangle=|\psi_{f_{1}}\rangle\otimes|\psi_{f_{2}}\rangle\otimes|\tilde{\Psi}\rangle, where |\tilde{\Psi}\rangle\in\mathscr{M}_{n-2,k-2}(d) is (k-2)-balanced, and |\psi_{f_{1}}\rangle,|\psi_{f_{2}}\rangle are two single-qudit magic states associated with cubic polynomials f_{1},f_{2} that have different cubic characters, which means \kappa(f_{1},\mathcal{T})\kappa(f_{2},\mathcal{T})\geq 1/(4d^{3}) for \mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d) thanks to Proposition 4 and Lemma 25 . By virtue of a similar reasoning employed above we can deduce that \displaystyle\kappa(\Psi,\mathcal{T})=\kappa(f_{1},\mathcal{T})\kappa(f_{2},\mathcal{T})\kappa(\tilde{\Psi},\mathcal{T})\geq\frac{1}{4d^{3}}\times\frac{\tilde{g}(d)^{2(k-2)/3}}{d^{4(k-2)/3}}\geq\frac{1}{4d^{(4k+1)/3}}\geq\frac{\kappa(\Psi,\Sigma(d))}{2D+2d}\quad\forall\mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d). (666) So Eq. ( 285 ) holds as before. ∎ L.7 Proof of Lemma 31 Proof of Lemma 31 . According to the definition in Eq. ( 278 ), if k \displaystyle\kappa_{d,

pa(f_{1},\mathcal{T})\kappa(f_{2},\mathcal{T})\geq 1/(4d^{3}) for \mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d) thanks to Proposition 4 and Lemma 25 . By virtue of a similar reasoning employed above we can deduce that \displaystyle\kappa(\Psi,\mathcal{T})=\kappa(f_{1},\mathcal{T})\kappa(f_{2},\mathcal{T})\kappa(\tilde{\Psi},\mathcal{T})\geq\frac{1}{4d^{3}}\times\frac{\tilde{g}(d)^{2(k-2)/3}}{d^{4(k-2)/3}}\geq\frac{1}{4d^{(4k+1)/3}}\geq\frac{\kappa(\Psi,\Sigma(d))}{2D+2d}\quad\forall\mathcal{T}\in\Sigma(d). (666) So Eq. ( 285 ) holds as before. ∎ L.7 Proof of Lemma 31 Proof of Lemma 31 . According to the definition in Eq. ( 278 ), if k \displaystyle\kappa_{d,k}=\frac{(s_{k}+1)\tilde{g}(d)^{2\lfloor k/3\rfloor}}{d^{k+\lfloor k/3\rfloor}}\geq\frac{1}{d^{n-1}}=\frac{d}{D}>\frac{3}{D+2}, (667) given that 0\leq s_{k}\leq 2 and 1\leq\tilde{g}(d)^{2} 235 ) (see also Lemma H2 in Appendix H ). Therefore, k_{*}(d,n)\geq 3n/4 according to the definition in Eq. ( 298 ), which confirms the first inequality in Eq. ( 300 ). The equality in Eq. ( 300 ) follows from Eqs. ( 278 ) and ( 298 ). The second and third inequalities in Eq. ( 300 ) follow from the fact that 1\leq\tilde{g}(d)^{2} If \tilde{g}(d)^{2}\geq 3d and n=2, then k_{*}(d,n)=n+1 by Eq. ( 299 ). If \tilde{g}(d)^{2}\geq 3d, n\geq 3, and k\leq n, then \displaystyle\kappa_{d,k}=\frac{(s_{k}+1)\tilde{g}(d)^{2\lfloor k/3\rfloor}}{d^{k+\lfloor k/3\rfloor}}\geq\

ion \tilde{g}(d)^{2}=1, then \kappa_{d,k}=(s_{k}+1)/d^{k+\lfloor k/3\rfloor} and Eq. ( 301 ) can be verified by direct calculation based on the definition in Eq. ( 298 ), which completes the proof of Lemma 31 . ∎ References [1] P. Delsarte, J. M. Goethals, and J. J. Seidel, Spherical codes and designs, Geom. Dedicata 6 , 363 (1977). [2] S. G.
